patronictl topology

Synopsis

topology
  [ CLUSTER_NAME [, ... ] ]
  [ --group CITUS_GROUP ]
  [ { -W | { -w | --watch } TIME } ]

Description

patronictl topology shows cluster and member information as a tree.

The following information is included in the output:

Cluster
Name of the Patroni cluster.

Note

Shown in the table header.

System identifier
Postgres system identifier.

Note

Shown in the table header.

Member
Name of the Patroni member.

Note

Information in this column is shown as a tree view of members in terms of replication connections.

Host
Host where the member is located.

Role
Current role of the member.

Can be one among:

  • Leader: the current leader of a regular Patroni cluster; or
  • Standby Leader: the current leader of a Patroni standby cluster; or
  • Sync Standby: a synchronous standby of a Patroni cluster with synchronous mode enabled; or
  • Replica: a regular standby of a Patroni cluster.

State
Current state of Postgres in the Patroni member.

Some examples among the possible states:

  • running: if Postgres is currently up and running;
  • streaming: if a replica and Postgres is currently streaming WALs from the primary node;
  • in archive recovery: if a replica and Postgres is currently fetching WALs from the archive;
  • stopped: if Postgres had been shut down;
  • crashed: if Postgres has crashed.

TL
Current Postgres timeline in the Patroni member.

Receive LSN
Last WAL location received through streaming replication and synchronized to disk: pg_catalog.pg_last_(xlog|wal)_receive_(location|lsn)().

Receive Lag
Replication lag between the Receive LSN position of the member and its upstream in MB.

Replay LSN
Last WAL location replayed during member recovery: pg_catalog.pg_last_(xlog|wal)_replay_(location|lsn)().

Replay Lag
Replication lag between the Replay LSN position of the member and its upstream in MB.

Besides that, the following information may be included in the output:

Group
Citus group ID.

Note

Shown in the table header.

Only shown if a Citus cluster.

Pending restart
* indicates the node needs a restart for some Postgres configuration to take effect. An empty value indicates the node does not require a restart.

Note

Shown as a member attribute.

Shown if node requires a restart.

Scheduled restart
Scheduled restart time for the member’s Postgres instance. Empty if no restart is scheduled.

Note

Shown as a member attribute.

Shown if node has a scheduled restart.

Tags
Member tags. Empty if no tags are set or all tags have default values.

Note

Shown as a member attribute.

Shown if node has any custom tags, or any default tags with non-default values.

Scheduled switchover
Timestamp at which a switchover has been scheduled for the Patroni cluster, if any.

Note

Shown in the table footer.

Only shown if there is a scheduled switchover.

Maintenance mode

If the cluster monitoring is currently paused.

Note

Shown in the table footer.

Only shown if the cluster is paused.

Parameters

CLUSTER_NAME
Name of the Patroni cluster.

If omitted, patronictl uses scope if configured.

--group
Show information about members from the given Citus group.

CITUS_GROUP is the ID of the Citus group.

-W
Automatically refresh information every 2 seconds.

-w / --watch
Automatically refresh information at the specified interval.

TIME is the interval between refreshes, in seconds.

Examples

Show topology of the cluster batman – postgresql1 and postgresql2 are replicating from postgresql0:

$ patronictl -c postgres0.yml topology batman
+ Cluster: batman (7277694203142172922) ---+-----------+----+-------------+-----+------------+-----+
| Member        | Host           | Role    | State     | TL | Receive LSN | Lag | Replay LSN | Lag |
+---------------+----------------+---------+-----------+----+-------------+-----+------------+-----+
| postgresql0   | 127.0.0.1:5432 | Leader  | running   |  8 |             |     |            |     |
| + postgresql1 | 127.0.0.1:5433 | Replica | streaming |  8 |   0/40004E8 |   0 |  0/40004E8 |   0 |
| + postgresql2 | 127.0.0.1:5434 | Replica | streaming |  8 |   0/40004E8 |   0 |  0/40004E8 |   0 |
+---------------+----------------+---------+-----------+----+-------------+-----+------------+-----+

6 - Replica imaging and bootstrap

Replica imaging, bootstrap, and custom replica creation workflows.

Source: https://patroni.readthedocs.io/en/latest/replica_bootstrap.html

Patroni supports custom replica creation and custom bootstrap for a new, empty cluster. These are separate operations. Patroni creates replicas only when the cluster has an initialize key in the DCS. Without that key, only the first node to acquire the initialize lock runs bootstrap.

Bootstrap

By default, Patroni initializes new clusters with PostgreSQL initdb. Some operations, such as copying an existing cluster, require a custom bootstrap method. Patroni can run a user-defined script and supply the cluster name and data directory. Configure this in the bootstrap section:

bootstrap:
    method: <custom_bootstrap_method_name>
    <custom_bootstrap_method_name>:
        command: <path_to_custom_bootstrap_script> [param1 [, ...]]
        keep_existing_recovery_conf: False
        no_params: False
        recovery_conf:
            recovery_target_action: promote
            recovery_target_timeline: latest
            restore_command: <method_specific_restore_command>

Each bootstrap method must define a name and a command. The special initdb method selects the default behavior. You can also omit method to use this default. Specify command as an absolute path or a path relative to the patroni command. In addition to fixed configuration parameters, Patroni supplies two cluster-specific arguments:

--scope
Name of the cluster to be bootstrapped

--datadir
Path to the data directory of the cluster instance to be bootstrapped

Set no_params to True to omit these two arguments.

If the script returns 0, Patroni tries to configure and start the PostgreSQL instance it created. Any intermediate failure or non-zero script result means bootstrap failed. Patroni cleans up and releases the initialize lock so another node can try bootstrap.

If the custom method includes recovery_conf, Patroni generates recovery.conf before it starts the new instance. On PostgreSQL >= 12, it sets recovery options in the Postgres configuration instead. Typically, specify at least one recovery_target_* parameter and set recovery_target_action to promote.

With keep_existing_recovery_conf set to True, Patroni keeps an existing recovery.conf on PostgreSQL <= 11. On PostgreSQL >= 12, it keeps existing recovery.signal and standby.signal files and does not override recovery settings. Use this with backup tools such as pgBackRest that generate recovery configuration.

Patroni passes additional key/value pairs from the custom method configuration to command as --name=value arguments. For example:

bootstrap:
    method: <custom_bootstrap_method_name>
    <custom_bootstrap_method_name>:
        command: <path_to_custom_bootstrap_script>
        arg1: value1
        arg2: value2

This adds --arg1=value1 --arg2=value2 when Patroni runs command.

Note

Patroni does not chain bootstrap methods. If the selected method fails, it does not fall back to the default method.

For example, bootstrap a new Patroni cluster from a Barman backup:

bootstrap:
    method: barman
    barman:
        keep_existing_recovery_conf: true
        command: patroni_barman --api-url https://barman-host:7480 recover
        barman-server: my_server
        ssh-command: ssh postgres@patroni-host
Note

Configure Barman and pg-backup-api on the Barman host before you use patroni_barman recover. It runs barman recover remotely through the backup API. The example shows only some available parameters. Run patroni_barman recover --help for details.

Building replicas

Patroni uses pg_basebackup to create replicas. This method requires a running leader. It has no backup compression during transfer or built-in cleanup of outdated backups. You may prefer WAL-E, pgBackRest, Barman, another backup tool, or a custom script. Patroni supports custom scripts to clone replicas. Configure them in the postgresql section:

postgresql:
    create_replica_methods:
        - <method name>
    <method name>:
        command: <command name>
        keep_data: True
        no_params: True
        no_leader: 1

example: wal_e

postgresql:
    create_replica_methods:
        - wal_e
        - basebackup
    wal_e:
        command: patroni_wale_restore
        no_leader: 1
        envdir: '{{WALE_ENV_DIR}}'
        use_iam: 1
    basebackup:
        max-rate: '100M'

example: pgbackrest

postgresql:
    create_replica_methods:
        - pgbackrest
        - basebackup
    pgbackrest:
        command: /usr/bin/pgbackrest --stanza=<scope> --delta restore
        keep_data: True
        no_params: True
    basebackup:
        max-rate: '100M'

example: Barman

postgresql:
    create_replica_methods:
        - barman
        - basebackup
    barman:
        command: patroni_barman --api-url https://barman-host:7480 recover
        barman-server: my_server
        ssh-command: ssh postgres@patroni-host
    basebackup:
        max-rate: '100M'
Note

Configure Barman and pg-backup-api on the Barman host before you use patroni_barman recover. It runs barman recover remotely through the backup API. The example shows only some available parameters. Run patroni_barman recover --help for details.

The create_replica_methods list specifies methods and their execution order. Patroni stops after the first method that returns 0. Give each method a separate configuration section with its command and custom parameters. Patroni passes parameters as --name=value arguments. In addition to custom parameters, it supplies these cluster-specific arguments:

--scope
Which cluster this replica belongs to

--datadir
Path to the data directory of the replica

--role
Always ‘replica’

--connstring
Connection string for the source cluster member, which can be the primary or another replica. The specified user can run SQL and replication protocol commands.

If no_leader is defined, Patroni can run the method without a running leader or replica. It passes an empty connection string. This permits restoration of a previous cluster from a binary backup.

If keep_data is defined, Patroni does not clean PGDATA before restore.

If no_params is defined, Patroni restricts parameters passed to the custom command.

The special basebackup method applies when create_replica_methods is empty. You can also list it explicitly. It initializes a replica with pg_basebackup. The backup source is the leader unless a replica has the clonefrom tag. If such replicas exist, Patroni selects one as the source.

No configuration is required, but you can add a basebackup section. As with other methods, specify only long options that start with –. Overriding the connection string or selecting tar or compressed backup output prevents Patroni from creating a replica. Patroni does not validate parameter names or values in basebackup.

If the WAL directory uses a symlink, specify the correct --waldir path. This preserves the symlink after replica creation or reinitialization. This option requires v10 or later.

Specify basebackup parameters as a key/value map or a list. Each list element can be a key/value pair or a single key for a valueless option, such as --verbose. For example:

postgresql:
    basebackup:
        max-rate: '100M'
        checkpoint: 'fast'

and

postgresql:
    basebackup:
        - verbose
        - max-rate: '100M'
        - waldir: /pg-wal-mount/external-waldir

If every method fails, Patroni retries them all in order during the next event loop cycle.

7 - Replication modes

Asynchronous and synchronous replication modes managed by Patroni.

Source: https://patroni.readthedocs.io/en/latest/replication_modes.html

Patroni uses PostgreSQL streaming replication. See the Postgres documentation. Patroni configures asynchronous replication by default. Select a replication design for your business requirements. Compare asynchronous replication, synchronous replication, and other HA solutions before you select one.

Asynchronous mode durability

Asynchronous mode permits some committed transaction loss to maintain availability. If the primary fails or becomes unavailable, Patroni automatically promotes a sufficiently healthy standby. Transactions absent from that standby remain on a forked timeline on the old primary. They are effectively unrecoverable1.

The maximum_lag_on_failover parameter controls potential transaction loss. Patroni does not sample the primary transaction log position in real time. The worst-case loss includes maximum_lag_on_failover bytes plus the log written during the last ttl seconds. The average additional interval is loop_wait/2 seconds. Typical replication delay in steady state is much less than one second.

By default, Patroni ignores replica timelines during leader elections. To require the same timeline as the former primary, set check_timeline to true. This prevents a replica on a different timeline from becoming leader.

PostgreSQL synchronous replication

Patroni supports Postgres synchronous replication. It confirms that a secondary has received writes before it returns success to the client. This maintains consistency across the cluster. It increases write latency and reduces write throughput. Throughput depends entirely on network performance.

On hosted networks, such as AWS or Rackspace, synchronous replication makes write performance much more variable. This also applies to other networks you do not control. If the leader cannot reach its replicas, it effectively becomes read-only.

For a simple synchronous replication test, add these lines to the parameters section of each YAML configuration file:

synchronous_commit: "on"
synchronous_standby_names: "*"

When using PostgreSQL synchronous replication, use at least three Postgres data nodes to ensure write availability if one host fails.

PostgreSQL synchronous replication does not always prevent transaction loss. If the primary and its synchronous replica fail together, Patroni promotes a third node. That node may lack some transactions.

Synchronous mode

If you cannot permit committed transaction loss, enable Patroni synchronous_mode. Patroni then promotes only a standby known to contain all transactions that may have returned successful commit status to clients2. Writes can become unavailable even while some servers remain available. Administrators can still promote a standby through manual failover, even if this loses transactions.

Synchronous mode does not always guarantee that commits are durable on multiple nodes. If no suitable standby exists, the primary still accepts writes without a replication guarantee. If the primary fails in this state, Patroni promotes no standby. When the former primary returns, Patroni promotes it automatically unless an administrator performed a manual failover. This behavior permits synchronous mode with two-node clusters.

With synchronous_mode, a standby crash blocks commits until the next Patroni iteration switches the primary to standalone mode. The worst-case write delay is ttl seconds; the average is loop_wait/2 seconds. Manual standby shutdowns and restarts do not interrupt commits. Before PostgreSQL shutdown, the standby tells the primary to release it from synchronous standby duties.

If each write must be durable on at least two nodes, also enable synchronous_mode_strict with synchronous_mode. Patroni then keeps synchronous replication enabled even if no synchronous standby candidate exists. Client writes block until at least one synchronous replica becomes available. A Postgres transaction that explicitly disables synchronous_commit is an exception.

When synchronous_mode_strict is enabled and no active replication connections satisfy the minimum replication factor, Patroni determines the value of synchronous_standby_names as follows:

  1. Last known sync nodes are available in the /sync key in DCS: Patroni uses the nodes stored there for synchronous_standby_names. For example, /sync may contain leader=node1, sync_standby=node2,node3. If both standbys stop streaming, Patroni continues to use:

    synchronous_standby_names = 'node2,node3'

    These nodes are the last ones known to have received the latest commit. Commits will block until at least one of them reconnects.

  2. Manual failover to an asynchronous node: A node outside /sync may be promoted, for example with patronictl failover --force. It sets synchronous_standby_names to the former primary. Only the former primary is guaranteed to have the latest committed data.

  3. The /sync key is empty: This can occur immediately after strict mode is enabled or after bootstrap without replicas. Patroni sets:

    synchronous_standby_names = '__patroni_strict_sync_replica_placeholder__'

    This sentinel value matches no real node name. Writes block until an eligible replica starts streaming from the primary. The placeholder replaces the old * wildcard, which could let an unqualified node satisfy the synchronous replication requirement.

Warning

The value __patroni_strict_sync_replica_placeholder__ is reserved by Patroni and must not be used as the name of a Patroni node in patroni.yaml. Patroni will refuse to start if this name is configured.

When strict mode is active, Patroni logs "No active replication connections and synchronous_mode_strict is requested. Commits will be delayed." It logs this warning once per activation, not on each HA loop iteration.

To prevent a standby from becoming synchronous, set its nosync tag to true. We recommend this for standbys on slow networks that would reduce synchronous replication performance. Setting nostream to true has the same effect.

Enable or disable synchronous mode with patronictl edit-config or the Patroni REST interface. See dynamic configuration.

Note: PostgreSQL synchronous replication can lose transactions even with synchronous_mode_strict. A backend can be cancelled while it waits for replication acknowledgment, through client timeout cancellation or backend failure. Its transaction changes then become visible to other backends before replication completes. These changes can be lost if a standby is promoted.

Synchronous Replication Factor

The synchronous_node_count parameter specifies the number of synchronous standbys. Its default is 1. It has no effect when synchronous_mode is off. Otherwise, Patroni updates DCS state and PostgreSQL synchronous_standby_names as members join or leave. If the configured count exceeds the number of eligible nodes, Patroni reduces it automatically.

Maximum lag on synchronous node

By default, Patroni retains nodes marked synchronous in pg_stat_replication, even if other nodes are ahead. This minimizes changes to synchronous_standby_names. Set maximum_lag_on_syncnode to change this behavior. It limits the replication lag permitted for a synchronous node.

With multiple standbys, Patroni uses the maximum replica LSN. With one standby, it uses the leader’s current WAL LSN. The default is -1. Values of 0 or less prevent Patroni from replacing an unhealthy synchronous standby. Use a sufficiently high value to avoid frequent replacements during high transaction volume.

Synchronous mode implementation

In synchronous mode, Patroni stores the latest primary and current synchronous standbys in the DCS /sync key. Strict update ordering maintains these invariants:

  • A node that can accept writes must be marked as the latest leader. Patroni crashes or PostgreSQL shutdown failures can violate this invariant.
  • A node listed as synchronous in the DCS /sync key must remain a synchronous standby in PostgreSQL.
  • Only the leader or a current synchronous standby can promote itself automatically.

Patroni assigns one or more synchronous standbys to synchronous_standby_names, according to synchronous_node_count.

Each HA loop iteration reevaluates synchronous standby selection. Connected synchronous standbys remain selected unless they request removal of synchronous status. Otherwise, Patroni selects eligible members that are furthest ahead in replication.

Example:

/config key in DCS

synchronous_mode: on
synchronous_node_count: 2
...

/sync key in DCS

{
    "leader": "node0",
    "sync_standby": "node1,node2"
}

postgresql.conf

synchronous_standby_names = 'FIRST 2 (node1,node2)'

In these examples, only node1 and node2 are known synchronous nodes. Only these nodes can be promoted automatically if the primary, node0, fails.

Quorum commit mode

Starting from PostgreSQL v10 Patroni supports quorum-based synchronous replication.

In this mode, Patroni stores synchronization state in the DCS. This includes the latest known primary, the quorum requirement, and eligible voters. In steady state, the voters are the leader and all synchronous standbys. Strict update ordering coordinates promotions and synchronous_standby_names. This guarantees that every subset of voters sufficient for quorum always includes a node with the latest successful commit.

Each HA loop iteration reevaluates synchronous standbys and quorum from node availability and requested cluster configuration. On PostgreSQL versions above 9.6, all eligible nodes become synchronous standbys as soon as they catch up to the leader.

Quorum commit reduces worst-case latency, including during normal operation. Other standbys can compensate for slow replication to one standby.

To enable quorum mode, set synchronous_mode to quorum with patronictl edit-config or the REST interface. See dynamic configuration.

Other parameters, like synchronous_node_count, maximum_lag_on_syncnode, and synchronous_mode_strict continue to work the same way as with synchronous_mode=on.

With quorum commit and synchronous_mode_strict, Patroni preserves the last known /sync voters when no active replica exists. It sets synchronous_standby_names to ANY N (<last known voters>). If /sync has no voters, it uses ANY 1 (__patroni_strict_sync_replica_placeholder__).

Example:

/config key in DCS

synchronous_mode: quorum
synchronous_node_count: 2
...

/sync key in DCS

{
    "leader": "node0",
    "sync_standby": "node1,node2,node3",
    "quorum": 1
}

postgresql.conf

synchronous_standby_names = 'ANY 2 (node1,node2,node3)'

If node0 fails in this example, two of node1, node2, and node3 have received the latest transaction. We do not know which two. To evaluate node1, compare its LSN with at least one of node2 or node3. This corresponds to quorum=1 in /sync. If node1 is not behind at least one of them, its promotion guarantees no user-visible data loss.

  1. The data remains, but data recovery specialists must recover it manually. With use_pg_rewind, Patroni automatically erases the forked timeline when the failed primary rejoins. For correct operation, use_pg_rewind requires data page checksums and/or wal_log_hints set to on. Enable checksums with the --data-checksums option for initdb. ↩︎

  2. Clients can change each transaction’s behavior with synchronous_commit. Values of off and local avoid replication delays but permit transaction loss on failover. ↩︎

8 - Standby cluster

Standby cluster setup, behavior, and replication from remote primary.

Source: https://patroni.readthedocs.io/en/latest/standby_cluster.html

Patroni supports cascading replication to a remote datacenter or region through the standby cluster feature. A standby cluster has:

  • A standby leader, which behaves like a regular cluster leader but replicates from a remote node.
  • Cascade replicas, which replicate from the standby leader.

The standby leader holds and updates a leader lock in the DCS. If the lock expires, the cascade replicas elect another leader from the standbys.

The standby and primary clusters share only replication information. If they use the same DCS, they must use different DCS scopes. The primary cluster does not show the standby cluster in patronictl_list or patronictl_topology output.

Use create_replica_methods in the standby_cluster section to specify replica creation and WAL recovery methods in standby mode. After the cluster detaches and operates normally, replica creation uses create_replica_methods in the postgresql section. Both standby and normal create_replica_methods refer to keys in the postgresql section.

To configure a standby cluster, add a standby_cluster section to the Patroni configuration:

bootstrap:
    dcs:
        standby_cluster:
            host: 1.2.3.4
            port: 5432
            primary_slot_name: patroni
            create_replica_methods:
            - basebackup

These options apply only during cluster bootstrap. After bootstrap, change them through the DCS.

Patroni expects postgresql.conf or postgresql.conf.backup in PGDATA on the remote primary. It will not start if this file is missing after a basebackup. If the primary stores postgresql.conf elsewhere, copy it to PGDATA.

If the standby cluster uses replication slots, create the corresponding slot on the primary cluster. The standby cluster does not create it automatically. You can use permanent replication slots on the primary cluster to maintain it. Use the name from primary_slot_name, or its default if you did not set primary_slot_name.

If the remote site has no single primary endpoint, list all source cluster hosts in standby_cluster.host. For multiple comma-separated hosts, Patroni will:

  • Add target_session_attrs=read-write to primary_conninfo on the standby leader.
  • Use target_session_attrs=read-write when it checks whether pg_rewind is necessary or runs pg_rewind on any standby cluster node.
  • Require data page checksums and/or wal_log_hints set to on for successful pg_rewind operation. Enable checksums with the --data-checksums option for initdb. Otherwise, pg_rewind cannot operate correctly.

To replicate from another standby cluster or a standby member of the primary cluster, specify one host in standby_cluster.host. However, pg_rewind will fail on the standby cluster in this configuration.

Warning

Member names must be unique across the primary cluster and all connected standby clusters. Set each member name in the name field of its Patroni configuration.

Patroni sets synchronous_standby_names on the primary with member names. These names also become the application_name of each replication connection in pg_stat_replication. Duplicate names create two connections with identical application_name values. PostgreSQL can then use a standby cluster connection to satisfy synchronous replication instead of the intended primary cluster member. It can acknowledge synchronous commits before transactions are durable on the correct standby.

This failure is silent: replication continues and logs contain no errors. The cluster effectively has no valid synchronous standby. Data loss can occur if the primary fails.

9 - Watchdog support

Watchdog integration and fencing considerations for Patroni clusters.

Source: https://patroni.readthedocs.io/en/latest/watchdog.html

Multiple PostgreSQL primaries can lose transactions because their timelines diverge. This is called split-brain. Patroni must prevent PostgreSQL from accepting transaction commits after the leader key expires in the DCS. Normally, Patroni stops PostgreSQL if the leader lock update fails for any reason. This protection can fail for these reasons:

  • Patroni crashes because of a bug, insufficient memory, or an accidental kill by a system administrator.
  • PostgreSQL shutdown takes too long.
  • System load, a VM pause by the hypervisor, or other infrastructure problems prevent Patroni from running.

Patroni supports watchdog devices to guarantee correct behavior under these conditions. A software or hardware watchdog resets the whole system if it receives no keepalive heartbeat within a specified time. This adds protection if the usual Patroni split-brain controls fail.

Patroni tries to activate the watchdog before it promotes PostgreSQL to primary. If activation fails and watchdog mode is required, the node refuses to become leader. Before it enters a leader election, Patroni checks whether the watchdog configuration permits it to become leader. After it demotes PostgreSQL, for example during manual failover, Patroni disables the watchdog. Patroni also disables the watchdog while paused.

By default, the watchdog expires 5 seconds before the TTL. With loop_wait=10 and ttl=30, the HA loop has at least 15 seconds before a forced reset. This interval is ttl - safety_margin - loop_wait. DCS access times out after 10 seconds by default. If the DCS is unavailable, for example because of network problems, Patroni and PostgreSQL have at least 5 seconds to terminate all client connections. This interval is ttl - safety_margin - loop_wait - retry_timeout.

The safety margin reserves time between a leader key update and a watchdog keepalive. Patroni tries to send the keepalive immediately after the DCS confirms the update. A long process suspension at this point can delay the keepalive beyond the safety margin without triggering the watchdog. The watchdog can then expire after the leader key, which invalidates the guarantee.

To guarantee watchdog expiration under all conditions, set safety_margin to -1. This sets the watchdog timeout to half the TTL: ttl // 2. If you need this guarantee, you probably should increase ttl and/or reduce loop_wait and retry_timeout.

Currently, Patroni supports only the Linux watchdog device interface.

Setting up software watchdog on Linux

On Linux, the default configuration tries to use /dev/watchdog if Patroni can access it. The Linux kernel software watchdog is sufficiently secure for most use cases.

Before you start Patroni, run these commands as root to enable the software watchdog:

modprobe softdog
# Replace postgres with the user you will be running patroni under
chown postgres /dev/watchdog

For tests, add soft_noboot=1 to the modprobe command to disable reboots. The watchdog then only writes to the kernel ring buffer. Use dmesg to read the message.

Patroni logs watchdog information after it enables the watchdog successfully.

10 - Pause/Resume mode for the cluster

Pause and resume mode behavior for Patroni cluster management.

Source: https://patroni.readthedocs.io/en/latest/pause.html

The goal

Patroni sometimes needs to suspend cluster management while it retains cluster state in the DCS. Examples include major version upgrades and corruption recovery. During these operations, nodes often start and stop for reasons that Patroni does not know. Temporary promotions can also violate the assumption that only one primary runs. Pause mode lets Patroni detach from the running cluster, like maintenance mode in Pacemaker.

The implementation

In pause mode, Patroni does not change PostgreSQL state, with these exceptions:

  • Patroni updates each node’s member key in the DCS with current cluster information. It runs read-only queries on each running member.
  • Patroni updates the leader lock for the Postgres primary that holds it. If that node is manually demoted, Patroni releases the lock instead of promoting the node again.
  • Manual unscheduled restarts, failovers, switchovers, and reinitialization are allowed. Scheduled actions are not allowed. A manual switchover must specify the target node.
  • If Patroni detects multiple primaries, it logs a warning. It does not demote a primary that lacks the leader lock.
  • If the cluster has no leader lock, a running primary acquires it. With multiple primaries, the first to acquire the lock wins. With no primary, Patroni does not promote a replica. One exception applies if the old primary released the lock by demoting itself for a manual promotion. Only the candidate named in that request may acquire the lock. After a new leader acquires the lock, Patroni redirects replicas from the previous leader to the new leader.
  • Patroni does not start a stopped Postgres instance. When Patroni stops, it does not stop the Postgres instance that it manages.
  • Patroni does not remove replication slots that do not represent another cluster member or are absent from the permanent slot configuration.

User guide

patronictl supports pause and resume commands.

You can also send a PATCH request to {namespace}/{cluster}/config with {"pause": true/false/null}.

11 - DCS Failsafe Mode

DCS failsafe mode behavior, requirements, and operational caveats.

Source: https://patroni.readthedocs.io/en/latest/dcs_failsafe_mode.html

The problem

Patroni uses the Distributed Configuration Store (DCS) for leader elections and network partition detection. A node can run Postgres as primary only if it can update the DCS leader lock. If the update fails, Patroni immediately demotes Postgres and starts it read-only. Failure frequency depends on the DCS. It is close to zero with Etcd dedicated to Patroni, but can be higher with the Etcd-backed K8s API.

Reasons for the current implementation

The leader lock update failure could be caused by two main reasons:

  1. Network partitioning
  2. DCS being down

A single node generally cannot distinguish these two causes. Patroni therefore assumes a network partition, the worst case. Other cluster nodes may acquire the leader lock and promote Postgres in a network partition. To prevent split-brain, Patroni demotes the old primary before the lock expires.

DCS Failsafe Mode

Enable failsafe_mode only through global dynamic configuration in the DCS /config key. In failsafe mode, a primary may continue after a leader lock update fails for reasons other than a version/value/index mismatch. It must be able to contact every known cluster member through the Patroni REST API.

Low-level implementation details

  • The DCS has a permanent /failsafe key.
  • This key contains all known members of the Patroni cluster at a given time.
  • The current leader maintains the /failsafe key.
  • A member can enter a leader race and become leader only if it is in the /failsafe key.
  • For a single-node cluster, the /failsafe key contains one member.
  • During a DCS outage, the primary contacts all members in /failsafe through the POST /failsafe REST API. It may remain primary if all replicas acknowledge it.
  • If any member does not respond, Patroni demotes the primary.
  • Replicas use incoming POST /failsafe requests to confirm that the primary is alive. They cache this information for ttl seconds.

F.A.Q.

  • Why MUST the current primary see ALL other members? Can’t we rely on quorum here?

    The DCS and Patroni can have different quorum views. DCS nodes must be distributed evenly across availability zones. Patroni has no equivalent rule or mechanism to enforce one. Most Patroni nodes, including the primary, could occupy the losing network partition while a minority occupies the winning partition. The primary must then be demoted. Only a check of ALL other members detects this case.

  • What if node/pod gets terminated while DCS is down?

    While the DCS is inaccessible, Patroni checks all other members every heartbeat cycle, or loop_wait seconds. If a node or pod terminates, the check fails. Patroni demotes Postgres to read-only. It cannot recover until DCS access returns.

  • What if all members of the Patroni cluster are lost while DCS is down?

    You can configure Patroni to create replicas from backup without a cluster leader. However, a new member absent from /failsafe cannot acquire the leader lock or promote.

  • What will happen if the primary lost access to DCS while replicas didn’t?

    The primary runs the failsafe code and contacts all known replicas. The replicas use this contact to confirm that the primary is alive. They do not start a leader race, even if the DCS leader lock expires.

  • How to enable the Failsafe Mode?

    Before you enable failsafe_mode, make sure all members have an up-to-date Patroni version. Then use the PATCH /config REST API or patronictl edit-config -s failsafe_mode=true.

12 - Using Patroni with Kubernetes

Using Patroni with Kubernetes objects, labels, and service discovery.

Source: https://patroni.readthedocs.io/en/latest/kubernetes.html

Patroni can store cluster state and the leader key in Kubernetes objects. This lets it run Postgres in Kubernetes without a separate consistency store, such as an extra Etcd deployment. Patroni supports two object types for the leader and configuration keys. Select the type with kubernetes.use_endpoints or the PATRONI_KUBERNETES_USE_ENDPOINTS environment variable.

Use Endpoints

This mode is recommended but disabled by default for compatibility. When enabled, Patroni creates Endpoints and stores cluster configuration and the leader key in their metadata: annotations fields. A leader change updates both the leader annotations and the addresses of the leader pod in one operation. This is safer than ConfigMaps.

Use ConfigMaps

In this mode, Patroni creates ConfigMaps and stores keys in their metadata. A leader change needs at least two updates: one for the leader ConfigMap and one for its Endpoint.

To route traffic to the Postgres leader, configure the Kubernetes Postgres service label selector with role_label from the Patroni configuration.

Some environments, such as OpenShift, require ConfigMaps.

Configuration

Patroni Kubernetes settings and environment variables are described in the general chapters of the documentation.

Customize role label

By default, Patroni labels its pod with the node role, such as role=primary. Customize the label key and values with kubernetes.role_label, kubernetes.leader_label_value, kubernetes.follower_label_value, and kubernetes.standby_leader_label_value.

To reduce downtime when you change default role labels to custom labels, use these steps:

  1. Set kubernetes.tmp_role_label to a temporary label, such as tmp_role, with the original role value. After the pods restart, Patroni sets these labels:
labels:
  cluster-name: foo
  role: primary
  tmp_role: primary
  1. After all pods have been updated, modify the service selector to select the temporary label.
selector:
  cluster-name: foo
  tmp_role: primary
  1. Add the custom role label, for example kubernetes.leader_label_value=primary. After the pods restart, Patroni sets these labels:
labels:
  cluster-name: foo
  role: primary
  tmp_role: primary
  1. After all pods have been updated again, modify the service selector to use new role value.
selector:
  cluster-name: foo
  role: primary
  1. Finally, remove the temporary label from your configuration and update all pods.
labels:
  cluster-name: foo
  role: primary

Examples

  • The kubernetes directory contains an example Docker image and Kubernetes manifest for tests. The current example cannot use PersistentVolumes because of permission problems.
  • The Spilo Project provides a full-featured Docker image with Persistent Volume support.
  • A Helm chart deploys the Spilo image with Patroni configured to run in Kubernetes.
  • For Patroni and Spilo clusters at scale, see postgres-operator. It uses the operator pattern to manage Spilo clusters.

13 - Citus support

Patroni integration details for Citus coordinator and worker groups.

Source: https://patroni.readthedocs.io/en/latest/citus.html

Patroni automates deployment of Multi-Node Citus clusters.

TL;DR

Apply these rules:

  1. Install the Citus PostgreSQL extension on every node. The minimum supported version is 10.0. We recommend at least 11.2 for transparent worker switchovers and restarts.
  2. Use the same cluster name (scope) on all Citus nodes.
  3. Use the same superuser credentials on the coordinator and all workers. Configure pg_hba.conf to permit superuser access between all nodes.
  4. Permit worker access to the coordinator REST API. Use the same credentials. If client certificates are configured, the coordinator must accept worker certificates.
  5. Add this section to patroni.yaml:
citus:
  group: X  # 0 for coordinator and 1, 2, 3, etc for workers
  database: citus  # must be the same on all nodes

Then start Patroni. It performs these operations:

  1. Set bootstrap.dcs.synchronous_mode to quorum, unless you explicitly set another value.
  2. Add the citus extension to shared_preload_libraries.
  3. Set max_prepared_transactions to 2*max_connections, unless it is explicitly set in dynamic configuration.
  4. Change citus.local_hostname from localhost to the address Patroni uses for local PostgreSQL connections. PostgreSQL might not listen on localhost.
  5. Create citus.database, then run CREATE EXTENSION citus.
  6. Add current superuser credentials to pg_dist_authinfo for cross-node communication. Update them if you later change the superuser username, password, sslcert, or sslkey.
  7. On the coordinator primary, discover worker primaries and add them to pg_dist_node with citus_add_node().
  8. Maintain pg_dist_node after failover or switchover in coordinator or worker clusters.

patronictl

Coordinator and worker clusters are separate PostgreSQL/Patroni clusters. Citus groups them logically. Most operations therefore cannot manage them as one entity.

A citus section in patroni.yaml changes patronictl behavior in two ways:

  1. By default, list and topology show all coordinators and workers in the Citus formation. The Group column identifies their Citus groups.
  2. patronictl commands accept --group. Some use a default from patroni.yaml. For example, patronictl_pause defaults to group in the citus section. patronictl_switchover and patronictl_remove require an explicit group.

An example of patronictl_list output for the Citus cluster:

postgres@coord1:~$ patronictl list demo
+ Citus cluster: demo ----------+----------------+---------+----+-------------+-----+------------+-----+
| Group | Member  | Host        | Role           | State   | TL | Receive LSN | Lag | Replay LSN | Lag |
+-------+---------+-------------+----------------+---------+----+-------------+-----+------------+-----+
|     0 | coord1  | 172.27.0.10 | Replica        | running |  1 |   0/41C0368 |   0 |  0/41C0368 |   0 |
|     0 | coord2  | 172.27.0.6  | Quorum Standby | running |  1 |   0/41C0368 |   0 |  0/41C0368 |   0 |
|     0 | coord3  | 172.27.0.4  | Leader         | running |  1 |             |     |            |     |
|     1 | work1-1 | 172.27.0.8  | Quorum Standby | running |  1 |   0/31D3198 |   0 |  0/31D3198 |   0 |
|     1 | work1-2 | 172.27.0.2  | Leader         | running |  1 |             |     |            |     |
|     2 | work2-1 | 172.27.0.5  | Quorum Standby | running |  1 |   0/31CDFC0 |   0 |  0/31CDFC0 |   0 |
|     2 | work2-2 | 172.27.0.7  | Leader         | running |  1 |             |     |            |     |
+-------+---------+-------------+----------------+---------+----+-------------+-----+------------+-----+

If we add the --group option, the output will change to:

postgres@coord1:~$ patronictl list demo --group 0
+ Citus cluster: demo (group: 0, 7179854923829112860) -+-------------+-----+------------+-----+
| Member | Host        | Role           | State   | TL | Receive LSN | Lag | Replay LSN | Lag |
+--------+-------------+----------------+---------+----+-------------+-----+------------+-----+
| coord1 | 172.27.0.10 | Replica        | running |  1 |   0/41C0368 |   0 |  0/41C0368 |   0 |
| coord2 | 172.27.0.6  | Quorum Standby | running |  1 |   0/41C0368 |   0 |  0/41C0368 |   0 |
| coord3 | 172.27.0.4  | Leader         | running |  1 |             |     |            |     |
+--------+-------------+----------------+---------+----+-------------+-----+------------+-----+

postgres@coord1:~$ patronictl list demo --group 1
+ Citus cluster: demo (group: 1, 7179854923881963547) -+-------------+-----+------------+-----+
| Member  | Host       | Role           | State   | TL | Receive LSN | Lag | Replay LSN | Lag |
+---------+------------+----------------+---------+----+-------------+-----+------------+-----+
| work1-1 | 172.27.0.8 | Quorum Standby | running |  1 |   0/31D3198 |   0 |  0/31D3198 |   0 |
| work1-2 | 172.27.0.2 | Leader         | running |  1 |             |     |            |     |
+---------+------------+----------------+---------+----+-------------+-----+------------+-----+

Citus worker switchover

Citus can make an orchestrated worker switchover nearly transparent to applications. Applications connect to the coordinator, which connects to workers. Citus can pause coordinator SQL traffic for the shards on the affected worker. The coordinator holds this traffic during switchover. Traffic resumes when the new worker primary can accept read/write queries.

An example of patronictl_switchover on the worker cluster:

postgres@coord1:~$ patronictl switchover demo
+ Citus cluster: demo ----------+----------------+---------+----+-------------+-----+------------+-----+
| Group | Member  | Host        | Role           | State   | TL | Receive LSN | Lag | Replay LSN | Lag |
+-------+---------+-------------+----------------+---------+----+-------------+-----+------------+-----+
|     0 | coord1  | 172.27.0.10 | Replica        | running |  1 |   0/41C0368 |   0 |  0/41C0368 |   0 |
|     0 | coord2  | 172.27.0.6  | Quorum Standby | running |  1 |   0/41C0368 |   0 |  0/41C0368 |   0 |
|     0 | coord3  | 172.27.0.4  | Leader         | running |  1 |             |     |            |     |
|     1 | work1-1 | 172.27.0.8  | Leader         | running |  1 |             |     |            |     |
|     1 | work1-2 | 172.27.0.2  | Quorum Standby | running |  1 |   0/31D3198 |   0 |  0/31D3198 |   0 |
|     2 | work2-1 | 172.27.0.5  | Quorum Standby | running |  1 |   0/31CDFC0 |   0 |  0/31CDFC0 |   0 |
|     2 | work2-2 | 172.27.0.7  | Leader         | running |  1 |             |     |            |     |

|     1 | work1-2 | 172.27.0.2  | Quorum Standby | running |  1 |   0/31D3198 |   0 |  0/31D3198 |   0 |
|     2 | work2-1 | 172.27.0.5  | Leader         | running |  2 |             |     |            |     |
|     2 | work2-2 | 172.27.0.7  | Quorum Standby | running |  2 |   0/31CDFC0 |   0 |  0/31CDFC0 |   0 |
+-------+---------+-------------+----------------+---------+----+-------------+-----+------------+-----+

And this is how it looks on the coordinator side:

# The worker primary notifies the coordinator that it is going to execute "pg_ctl stop".
2024-08-26 07:02:38,636 DEBUG: query(BEGIN, ())
2024-08-26 07:02:38,636 DEBUG: query(SELECT pg_catalog.citus_update_node(%s, %s, %s, true, %s), (3, '172.19.0.7-demoted', 5432, 10000))
# From this moment all application traffic on the coordinator to the worker group 2 is paused.

# The old worker primary is assigned as a secondary.
2024-08-26 07:02:40,084 DEBUG: query(SELECT pg_catalog.citus_update_node(%s, %s, %s, true, %s), (7, '172.19.0.7', 5432, 10000))

# The future worker primary notifies the coordinator that it acquired the leader lock in DCS and about to run "pg_ctl promote".
2024-08-26 07:02:40,085 DEBUG: query(SELECT pg_catalog.citus_update_node(%s, %s, %s, true, %s), (3, '172.19.0.5', 5432, 10000))

# The new worker primary just finished promote and notifies coordinator that it is ready to accept read-write traffic.
2024-08-26 07:02:41,485 DEBUG: query(COMMIT, ())
# From this moment the application traffic on the coordinator to the worker group 2 is unblocked.

Secondary nodes

Since Patroni v4.0.0, Citus secondary nodes without the noloadbalance tag are also registered in pg_dist_node. To use them for read-only queries, applications must change citus.use_secondary_nodes.

Peek into DCS

The Citus cluster (coordinator and workers) are stored in DCS as a fleet of Patroni clusters logically grouped together:

/service/batman/              # scope=batman
/service/batman/0/            # citus.group=0, coordinator
/service/batman/0/initialize
/service/batman/0/leader
/service/batman/0/members/
/service/batman/0/members/m1
/service/batman/0/members/m2
/service/batman/1/            # citus.group=1, worker
/service/batman/1/initialize
/service/batman/1/leader
/service/batman/1/members/
/service/batman/1/members/m3
/service/batman/1/members/m4
...

Most DCS implementations can read the whole Citus cluster with one recursive request using this structure. Only coordinators read the whole tree, to discover workers. Workers read their own group subtree and, in some cases, the coordinator subtree.

Citus on Kubernetes

Kubernetes does not support hierarchical structures. Patroni therefore includes the Citus group in every Kubernetes object name:

batman-0-leader  # the leader config map for the coordinator
batman-0-config  # the config map holding initialize, config, and history "keys"
...
batman-1-leader  # the leader config map for worker group 1
batman-1-config
...

The naming pattern is ${scope}-${citus.group}-${type}.

Patroni discovers Kubernetes objects with a label selector. All Patroni/Citus Pods and Endpoints/ConfigMaps must have matching labels. Configure Patroni to use these labels through Kubernetes settings or environment variables <kubernetes_environment>.

A couple of examples of Patroni configuration using Pods environment variables:

  1. for the coordinator cluster
apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  labels:
    application: patroni
    citus-group: "0"
    citus-type: coordinator
    cluster-name: citusdemo
  name: citusdemo-0-0
  namespace: default
spec:
  containers:
  - env:
    - name: PATRONI_SCOPE
      value: citusdemo
    - name: PATRONI_NAME
      valueFrom:
        fieldRef:
          apiVersion: v1
          fieldPath: metadata.name
    - name: PATRONI_KUBERNETES_POD_IP
      valueFrom:
        fieldRef:
          apiVersion: v1
          fieldPath: status.podIP
    - name: PATRONI_KUBERNETES_NAMESPACE
      valueFrom:
        fieldRef:
          apiVersion: v1
          fieldPath: metadata.namespace
    - name: PATRONI_KUBERNETES_LABELS
      value: '{application: patroni}'
    - name: PATRONI_CITUS_DATABASE
      value: citus
    - name: PATRONI_CITUS_GROUP
      value: "0"
  1. for the worker cluster from the group 2
apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  labels:
    application: patroni
    citus-group: "2"
    citus-type: worker
    cluster-name: citusdemo
  name: citusdemo-2-0
  namespace: default
spec:
  containers:
  - env:
    - name: PATRONI_SCOPE
      value: citusdemo
    - name: PATRONI_NAME
      valueFrom:
        fieldRef:
          apiVersion: v1
          fieldPath: metadata.name
    - name: PATRONI_KUBERNETES_POD_IP
      valueFrom:
        fieldRef:
          apiVersion: v1
          fieldPath: status.podIP
    - name: PATRONI_KUBERNETES_NAMESPACE
      valueFrom:
        fieldRef:
          apiVersion: v1
          fieldPath: metadata.namespace
    - name: PATRONI_KUBERNETES_LABELS
      value: '{application: patroni}'
    - name: PATRONI_CITUS_DATABASE
      value: citus
    - name: PATRONI_CITUS_GROUP
      value: "2"

Both examples set the citus-group label to identify the object’s Citus group. They set PATRONI_CITUS_GROUP to the same value. Patroni automatically adds citus-group: ${env.PATRONI_CITUS_GROUP} to new ConfigMaps and Endpoints:

apiVersion: v1
kind: ConfigMap
metadata:
  name: citusdemo-0-leader  # Is generated as ${env.PATRONI_SCOPE}-${env.PATRONI_CITUS_GROUP}-leader
  labels:
    application: patroni    # Is set from the ${env.PATRONI_KUBERNETES_LABELS}
    cluster-name: citusdemo # Is automatically set from the ${env.PATRONI_SCOPE}
    citus-group: '0'        # Is automatically set from the ${env.PATRONI_CITUS_GROUP}

See the kubernetes directory for a complete Patroni/Citus deployment example.

There are two important files for you:

  1. Dockerfile.citus
  2. citus_k8s.yaml

Citus upgrades and PostgreSQL major upgrades

Read the Citus upgrade documentation. One change applies: restart PostgreSQL with patronictl_restart instead of systemctl restart.

A PostgreSQL major upgrade with Citus requires both projects’ upgrade procedures. Combine the Citus major upgrade documentation with Patroni PostgreSQL major upgrade<major_upgrade> instructions. A Citus formation contains multiple Patroni clusters: the coordinator and workers. Upgrade each cluster independently.

14 - Convert a Standalone to a Patroni Cluster

Procedure to convert existing PostgreSQL data into a Patroni cluster.

Source: https://patroni.readthedocs.io/en/latest/existing_data.html

This section describes the process for converting a standalone PostgreSQL instance into a Patroni cluster.

To deploy a Patroni cluster without using a pre-existing PostgreSQL instance, see Running and Configuring instead.

Procedure

The following steps convert an existing Postgres cluster to Patroni management. All nodes must be running. These steps assume you do not intend to change the Postgres configuration during migration.

  1. Create the Postgres users described in the authentication section. In the sample SQL below, replace usernames and passwords for your environment. Skip this step if the required users already exist.

    -- Patroni superuser
-- Replace PATRONI_SUPERUSER_USERNAME and PATRONI_SUPERUSER_PASSWORD accordingly
CREATE USER PATRONI_SUPERUSER_USERNAME WITH SUPERUSER ENCRYPTED PASSWORD 'PATRONI_SUPERUSER_PASSWORD';

-- Patroni replication user
-- Replace PATRONI_REPLICATION_USERNAME and PATRONI_REPLICATION_PASSWORD accordingly
CREATE USER PATRONI_REPLICATION_USERNAME WITH REPLICATION ENCRYPTED PASSWORD 'PATRONI_REPLICATION_PASSWORD';

-- Patroni rewind user, if you intend to enable use_pg_rewind in your Patroni configuration
-- Replace PATRONI_REWIND_USERNAME and PATRONI_REWIND_PASSWORD accordingly
CREATE USER PATRONI_REWIND_USERNAME WITH ENCRYPTED PASSWORD 'PATRONI_REWIND_PASSWORD';
GRANT EXECUTE ON function pg_catalog.pg_ls_dir(text, boolean, boolean) TO PATRONI_REWIND_USERNAME;
GRANT EXECUTE ON function pg_catalog.pg_stat_file(text, boolean) TO PATRONI_REWIND_USERNAME;
GRANT EXECUTE ON function pg_catalog.pg_read_binary_file(text) TO PATRONI_REWIND_USERNAME;
GRANT EXECUTE ON function pg_catalog.pg_read_binary_file(text, bigint, bigint, boolean) TO PATRONI_REWIND_USERNAME;

  2. Complete these steps on each Postgres node. Finish one node before you continue to the next. Start with the primary, then process each standby:

    1. If systemd runs Postgres, disable the Postgres systemd unit. Patroni will manage Postgres startup and shutdown.
    2. Create a Patroni YAML configuration file. You can use Patroni configuration generation and validation tooling.
      • Note (specific for the primary node): If cluster members use replication slots, we recommend enabling use_slots. Configure the existing slots as permanent with slots. When use_slots is enabled, Patroni creates member replication slots and drops slots it does not recognize. Permanent slot configuration preserves your existing slots during migration. See Dynamic Configuration Settings for details.
    3. Start the patroni systemd service. Patroni detects that Postgres is running and starts monitoring it.

  3. Transfer Postgres startup management to Patroni. Restart members with patronictl restart cluster-name member-name. To minimize downtime, you may split this step:

    1. Immediate restart of the standby nodes.
    2. Scheduled restart of the primary node within a maintenance window.

  4. If you configured permanent slots in step 1.2., wait for the Patroni slots to catch up. Their restart_lsn must reach the restart_lsn of the corresponding original slots. Then remove the original slots from slots with patronictl edit-config cluster-name. Patroni can then drop the original slots when they are no longer needed. Use this example query to compare restart_lsn values:

    -- Assume original_slot_for_member_x is the name of the slot in your original
-- cluster for replicating changes to member X, and slot_for_member_x is the
-- slot created by Patroni for that purpose. You need restart_lsn of
-- slot_for_member_x to be >= restart_lsn of original_slot_for_member_x
SELECT slot_name,
       restart_lsn
FROM pg_replication_slots
WHERE slot_name IN (
    'original_slot_for_member_x',
    'slot_for_member_x'
)

Major Upgrade of PostgreSQL Version

The only possible way to do a major upgrade currently is:

  1. Stop Patroni.
  2. Upgrade the PostgreSQL binaries. Run pg_upgrade on the primary.
  3. Update patroni.yml.
  4. Remove the initialize key from the DCS, or remove all cluster state with patronictl remove cluster-name. This is necessary because pg_upgrade runs initdb, which creates a database with a new PostgreSQL system identifier.
  5. If you removed all cluster state, you may copy patroni.dynamic.json from the old data directory to the new directory. This retains some PostgreSQL parameters that you set earlier.
  6. Start Patroni on the primary.
  7. On standby nodes, upgrade PostgreSQL binaries, update patroni.yml, and remove the contents of data_dir.
  8. Start Patroni on the standbys. Wait for replication to complete.

PostgreSQL does not support pg_upgrade on standby nodes. If you understand the procedure, you can use rsync as described in https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/pgupgrade.html instead of removing data_dir. Letting Patroni replicate the data is the safest method.

FAQ

  • During Patroni startup, Patroni complains that it cannot bind to the PostgreSQL port.

    Check listen_addresses and port in postgresql.conf, and postgresql.listen in patroni.yml. Make sure pg_hba.conf permits this access.

  • After asking Patroni to restart the node, PostgreSQL displays the error message could not open configuration file "/etc/postgresql/10/main/pg_hba.conf": No such file or directory

    The cause depends on how you manage PostgreSQL configuration. With postgresql.config_dir, Patroni generates pg_hba.conf from bootstrap settings only during new-cluster bootstrap. Here, PGDATA was not empty, so no bootstrap occurred. The file must already exist.

15 - Integration with other tools

Integrating Patroni with external backup and orchestration tools.

Source: https://patroni.readthedocs.io/en/latest/tools_integration.html

Patroni can integrate with other tools in your stack. These examples show possible integrations. The list is not complete.

Barman

Patroni provides patroni_barman. This application communicates with pg-backup-api to run Barman operations remotely.

The application has two subcommands: recover and config-switch.

patroni_barman recover

Use the recover subcommand as a custom bootstrap method or replica creation method. See replica_imaging_and_bootstrap for details.

patroni_barman config-switch

Use the config-switch subcommand as an on_role_change callback in Patroni. For example, you may stream WALs from the primary to your Barman host. After a failover, you may want to stream from the new primary. Use patroni_barman config-switch as the on_role_change callback for this change.

Note

This subcommand uses barman config-switch, available since Barman 3.10. The command overrides a Barman server configuration with a predefined model. See the Barman documentation for details.

This example applies a configuration model when the Patroni node becomes primary:

postgresql:
    callbacks:
        on_role_change: >
            patroni_barman
                --api-url YOUR_API_URL
                config-switch
                --barman-server YOUR_BARMAN_SERVER_NAME
                --barman-model YOUR_BARMAN_MODEL_NAME
                --switch-when promoted
Note

Configure Barman and pg-backup-api on the Barman host before you use patroni_barman config-switch. This lets it run barman config-switch remotely through the backup API. You must also configure the Barman models in advance. The example uses only some available parameters. Run patroni_barman config-switch --help or see the Barman documentation for details.

16 - Security Considerations

Security considerations for DCS, REST API, and credential handling.

Source: https://patroni.readthedocs.io/en/latest/security.html

Protect two Patroni cluster interfaces from unauthorized access: the distributed configuration storage (DCS) and the Patroni REST API.

Protecting DCS

Patroni and patronictl store data in the DCS and retrieve data from it.

The DCS contains no sensitive information, but it permits changes to some Patroni/Postgres settings. Protect the DCS first.

Protection depends on the DCS type. See settings for authentication and encryption parameters: tokens, basic authentication, and client certificates.

The general recommendation is to enable TLS for all DCS communication.

Protecting the REST API

Protecting the REST API is a more complicated task.

Patroni uses its REST API during leader races. The patronictl tool uses it for failovers, switchovers, reinitialization, restarts, and reloads. HAProxy and other load balancers use it for HTTP health checks. You can also use it for monitoring.

The REST API has safe endpoints (GET requests) that only retrieve information. Unsafe endpoints (PUT, POST, PATCH and DELETE requests) change node state.

To protect unsafe endpoints with HTTP basic authentication, set restapi.authentication.username and restapi.authentication.password. Safe endpoints require TLS for protection.

With TLS enabled and a PKI established, API servers and clients can authenticate each other for all endpoints.

The restapi section enables TLS client authentication. The verify_client parameter specifies which calls require successful client certificate verification. Use verify_client: required for all calls, verify_client: optional for unsafe calls only, or verify_client: none for no calls.

The ctl section enables TLS server authentication for the client. The client, patronictl, uses the same configuration as Patroni. Set insecure: true to disable server certificate verification by the client. See settings for TLS client parameters.

PostgreSQL database access protection is outside this document. See https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/client-authentication.html.

17 - HA multi datacenter

Multi-datacenter high-availability patterns with Patroni replication.

Source: https://patroni.readthedocs.io/en/latest/ha_multi_dc.html

A PostgreSQL cluster across multiple data centers uses replication for high availability. Replication can be synchronous or asynchronous. See replication modes.

Both modes require these considerations:

  • Postgres can run as primary or standby leader only if it owns and can update the leader key.
  • Use an odd number of etcd, ZooKeeper, or Consul nodes: 3 or 5.

Synchronous Replication

A multi-datacenter cluster needs at least 3 zones to tolerate a zone failure automatically.

The architecture diagram would be the following:

Deploy at least 3 etcd, ZooKeeper, or Consul nodes across the data centers, with one node in each zone.

Deploy at least 2 Postgres nodes in different data centers. Set synchronous_mode: true in the global dynamic configuration.

This enables synchronous replication. The primary selects one node as its synchronous standby.

Asynchronous Replication

With two data centers, use two independent etcd clusters and a Patroni standby cluster in the second data center. This is the preferred arrangement. If the first site fails, you can manually promote the standby_cluster.

The architecture diagram would be the following:

Automatic promotion is not possible because DC2 cannot determine the state of DC1.

Do not use pg_ctl promote in this scenario. To promote the healthy cluster manually, remove standby_cluster from the dynamic configuration.

Warning

If the source cluster is still up and running and you promote the standby cluster you create a split-brain.

There are two ways to restore the initial state:

  • Add the standby_cluster section again to trigger pg_rewind. For correct operation, pg_rewind requires data page checksums and/or wal_log_hints set to on. Enable checksums with the --data-checksums option for initdb. Other factors can still cause pg_rewind to fail.
  • Rebuild the standby cluster.

Before you promote the standby cluster, manually ensure that the source cluster is down (STONITH). When DC1 recovers, convert its cluster to a standby cluster.

Before conversion, you may inspect the database manually. Extract changes made between the network failure between DC1 and DC2 and the manual shutdown of the DC1 cluster.

You may then apply these changes manually to the DC2 cluster.

18 - FAQ

Frequently asked questions about Patroni operation and troubleshooting.

Source: https://patroni.readthedocs.io/en/latest/faq.html

This section answers common Patroni questions. Each subsection covers a different topic.

For more help or unexpected problems, see chatting and reporting_bugs.

Comparison with other HA solutions

Why does Patroni require a separate cluster of DCS nodes while other solutions like repmgr do not?
There are different ways of implementing HA solutions, each of them with their pros and cons.

Software like repmgr performs communication among the nodes to decide when actions should be taken.

Patroni uses DCS state as the authoritative source for its decisions.

A separate DCS cluster adds architectural complexity but reduces the risk of split-brain in your Postgres cluster.

What is the difference between Patroni and other HA solutions in regards to Postgres management?
Patroni does not just manage the high availability of the Postgres cluster but also manages Postgres itself.

If Postgres nodes do not exist, Patroni bootstraps the primary and standbys and manages their configuration. For existing nodes, Patroni takes over cluster management.

Patroni also attempts automatic recovery. After primary failure, it fails over to a replica and tries to rejoin the old primary as a replica. After replica failure, it tries to rejoin that replica.

Patroni is a template for HA solutions because it manages Postgres as a whole, including physical replication.

DCS

Can I use the same etcd cluster to store data from two or more Patroni clusters?
Yes, you can!

Information about a Patroni cluster is stored in the DCS under a path prefixed with the namespace and scope Patroni settings.

Multiple Patroni clusters can share a DCS cluster if their namespace and scope combinations are distinct.

What occurs if I attempt to use the same combination of namespace and scope for different Patroni clusters that point to the same DCS cluster?
The second cluster finds existing DCS information with an incompatible Postgres system identifier. Patroni interprets this as a different cluster and a configuration error. It stops management of the second cluster.

Make sure to use different namespace / scope when dealing with different Patroni clusters that share the same DCS cluster.

What occurs if I lose my DCS cluster?
The DCS is used to store basically status and the dynamic configuration of the Patroni cluster.

All Patroni clusters that use the lost DCS enter read-only mode unless dcs_failsafe_mode is enabled.

What should I do if I lose my DCS cluster?
There are three possible outcomes upon losing your DCS cluster:

  1. The DCS cluster recovers fully. Patroni needs no action and should recover after DCS recovery.
  2. The DCS cluster is recreated with the same endpoints. Patroni needs no configuration changes.
  3. A new DCS cluster has different endpoints. Update the DCS endpoints in every Patroni node’s configuration.

In scenarios 2. and 3., Patroni recreates DCS status information from current cluster state. It restores dynamic configuration from patroni.dynamic.json. Each Patroni member stores this backup in its Postgres data directory.

What occurs if I lose majority in my DCS cluster?
The DCS will become unresponsive, which will cause Patroni to demote the current read/write Postgres node.

Remember: Patroni relies on the state of the DCS to take actions on the cluster.

You can use the dcs_failsafe_mode to alleviate that situation.

patronictl

Do I need to run patronictl in the Patroni host?
No, you do not need to do that.

On a Patroni host, patronictl can use the same configuration file as the patroni agent. This is convenient if you have access to the host.

patronictl is a client and can run remotely. Configure it to reach the DCS and the REST APIs of the required Patroni members.

Why did the information from one of my Patroni members disappear from the output of patronictl_list command?
Information shown by patronictl_list is based on the contents of the DCS.

If a member disappears from the DCS, its Patroni agent probably stopped or cannot communicate with the DCS.

Without updates, member information expires from the DCS. The member then disappears from patronictl_list output.

Why is the information about one of my Patroni members not up-to-date in the output of patronictl_list command?
Information shown by patronictl_list is based on the contents of the DCS.

Patroni normally updates this information approximately every loop_wait seconds. Even during normal operation, DCS information can therefore lag by up to loop_wait seconds.

This interval is not fixed. Some Patroni operations update DCS information immediately.

Configuration

What is the difference between dynamic configuration and local configuration?
Dynamic, or global, configuration is stored in the DCS and applies to all cluster members. Store most configuration there.

Use local configuration for node-specific settings or overrides of global settings. Configure only the required member, through its configuration file or environment variables.

See more in config.

What are the types of configuration in Patroni, and what is the precedence?
The types are:

  • Dynamic configuration: applied to all members;
  • Local configuration: applied to the local member, overrides dynamic configuration;
  • Environment configuration: applied to the local member, overrides both dynamic and local configuration.

Note: Some Postgres GUCs can only be set globally through dynamic configuration. Patroni also enforces hard-coded values for some GUCs.

See more in config.

Is there any facility to help me create my Patroni configuration file?
Yes, there is.

Use patroni --generate-sample-config to generate a sample configuration. Use patroni --generate-config to generate configuration from an existing Postgres instance.

Please refer to generate_sample_config and generate_config for more details.

I changed my parameters under bootstrap.dcs configuration but Patroni is not applying the changes to the cluster members. What is wrong?
Patroni uses bootstrap.dcs only to bootstrap a new cluster. During bootstrap, it writes these values to the DCS.

After bootstrap, change dynamic configuration through the DCS.

Refer to the next question for more details.

How can I change my dynamic configuration?
Change DCS configuration with either method:

  • patronictl_edit_config; or
  • A PATCH request to config_endpoint.

How can I change my local configuration?
Edit the member’s configuration file and signal its Patroni agent with SIHGUP. Use one of these methods:

  • Send a POST request to the REST API reload_endpoint; or

  • Run patronictl_reload; or

  • Locally signal the Patroni process with SIGHUP:

    • With systemd, run systemctl reload PATRONI_UNIT.service. Replace PATRONI_UNIT with the Patroni service name.
    • Otherwise, find the patroni process and run kill -s HUP PID. Replace PID with its process ID.

Note: patronictl_reload can fail in these cases:

  • Expired REST API certificates: use the -k option of patronictl to bypass this problem.
  • Wrong credentials: for example, you changed restapi or ctl credentials in a file shared by Patroni and patronictl.

How can I change my environment configuration?
The environment configuration is only read by Patroni during startup.

After an environment configuration change, restart the corresponding Patroni agent.

Avoid triggering failover during the restart. See patronictl_pause.

How can I reduce repetitive heartbeat log lines during normal operation?

To suppress repeated lines such as Lock owner: ... and no action. I am ..., set log.deduplicate_heartbeat_logs: true.

You can set it either in the Patroni YAML file (log settings) or with PATRONI_LOG_DEDUPLICATE_HEARTBEAT_LOGS=true.

This reduces log volume. It also removes per-loop heartbeat information that can help diagnose failover.

What occurs if I change a Postgres GUC that requires a reload?
After a dynamic or local configuration change, Patroni reloads the Postgres configuration.

What occurs if I change a Postgres GUC that requires a restart?
Patroni will mark the affected members with a flag of pending restart.

Choose when and how to restart affected members. Use either method:

  • patronictl_restart; or
  • A POST request to restart_endpoint.

Note: Some Postgres GUCs require a specific node restart order. See shared_memory_gucs.

What is the difference between etcd and etcd3 in Patroni configuration?
etcd uses the API version 2 of etcd, while etcd3 uses the API version 3 of etcd.

API version 3 cannot manage data stored through version 2, and version 2 cannot manage data stored through version 3.

We recommend that you configure etcd3 instead of etcd because:

  • API version 2 is disabled by default from Etcd v3.4 onward;
  • API version 2 will be completely removed on Etcd v3.6.

I enabled use_slots in Patroni. When a member stays offline for some time, the upstream node drops its replication slot. How can I prevent this? There are two options:

  1. Adjust member_slots_ttl, available since Patroni 4.0.0 with PostgreSQL 11 or later. The default is 30min. Patroni retains an absent member’s slot while its downtime is shorter than this threshold.
  2. Configure permanent physical replication slots for the members.

Since Patroni 3.2.0, member slots can be permanent slots managed by Patroni.

Patroni creates permanent physical slots on all nodes and retains them. It advances each slot’s LSN to the LSN consumed by its member.

If you remove a member later, you must adjust permanent slot configuration. Otherwise, Patroni retains its slots indefinitely.

Note: Before Patroni 3.2.0, permanent physical member slots were managed only on the current leader. After failover or switchover, Patroni created them on the new leader. This did not guarantee that the new leader had all WAL segments required by an absent node.

Note: Patroni 3.2.0 can still have a small race condition. A new replica slot can initially be ahead of the corresponding leader slot. If nobody consumes the slot, some files can still be missing after failover. We recommend continuous archiving so you can restore required WALs or perform PITR.

What is the difference between loop_wait, retry_timeout and ttl?
Patroni regularly runs an HA cycle to check cluster health. It may then take action, such as failover to a standby.

loop_wait is the delay in seconds before Patroni starts the next HA cycle.

retry_timeout sets the timeout for DCS and Postgres retries. If the DCS remains unresponsive beyond retry_timeout seconds, Patroni may demote the primary as a protective action.

ttl is the lease duration for the DCS leader lock. The leader renews it during HA cycles. If it cannot renew the lease for longer than ttl, the lock expires and starts a leader race.

Note: Patroni enforces minimum values and a timing rule for these settings. See dynamic.

Postgres management

Can I change Postgres GUCs directly in Postgres configuration?
You can, but you should avoid that.

Patroni manages Postgres configuration. It may overwrite changes that you make directly in configuration files.

To manage settings outside Patroni, use one of these methods:

  • Change Postgres GUCs through $PGDATA/postgresql.base.conf; or
  • Define a postgresql.custom_conf which will be used instead of postgresql.base.conf so you can manage that externally; or
  • Change GUCs using ALTER SYSTEM / ALTER DATABASE / ALTER USER.

You can find more information about that in the section important_configuration_rules.

We recommend managing all Postgres configuration through Patroni. This centralizes management and makes troubleshooting easier.

Can I restart Postgres nodes directly?
No, you should not attempt to manage Postgres directly!

Restarting Postgres outside Patroni can trigger failover.

Use the management operations that Patroni provides.

Is Patroni able to take over management of an already existing Postgres cluster?
Yes, it can!

Please refer to existing_data for detailed instructions.

How does Patroni manage Postgres?
Patroni starts and stops Postgres with binaries such as pg_ctl and postgres.

You MUST disable other Postgres management services, such as postgresql.service. Only Patroni should start, stop, or promote cluster instances. Other services can cause split-brain. For example, after primary failure, an enabled postgresql.service can restart Postgres independently and create a second primary.

Concepts and requirements

Which are the applications that make part of Patroni?
Patroni provides these applications:

  • patroni: The agent that manages a Postgres node.
  • patronictl: The command-line client for switchovers, restarts, configuration changes, and other cluster operations. See patronictl for details.

What is a standby cluster in Patroni?
A standby cluster has no running primary Postgres node and no read/write member.

Standby clusters replicate data from another cluster, often across data centers.

The standby leader replicates from a remote Postgres node. Other standbys use cascading replication from that leader.

Note: The standby cluster has no other knowledge of its source cluster. It can use restore_command instead of WAL streaming and an independent DCS cluster.

Refer to standby_cluster for more details.

What is a leader in Patroni?
A leader in Patroni is like a coordinator of the cluster.

In a regular Patroni cluster, the leader will be the read/write node.

In a standby cluster, the leader, also called standby leader, replicates from a remote Postgres node. It cascades those changes to other standby cluster members.

Does Patroni require a minimum number of Postgres nodes in the cluster?
No, you can run Patroni with any number of Postgres nodes.

Remember: Patroni is decoupled from the DCS.

What does pause mean in Patroni?
Pause suspends Patroni management of Postgres.

Use pause during maintenance to prevent automatic HA actions, such as failover when you stop the primary.

You can find more information about that in pause.

Automatic failover

How does the automatic failover mechanism of Patroni work?
Patroni automatic failover is based on what we call leader race.

Patroni stores cluster status in the DCS. The leader lock contains the name of the current leader.

The leader lock has a time-to-live. If the leader does not renew its lease in time, the DCS key expires.

When the leader lock expires, a leader race starts. All nodes check whether they are the best candidate for the leader role. Some checks call the REST APIs of all other Patroni members.

Each member that considers itself the best candidate tries to acquire the leader lock. The first to acquire it becomes the read/write node or standby leader. Patroni configures the other members to follow it.

Can I temporarily disable automatic failover in the Patroni cluster?
Yes, you can!

Temporarily pause the cluster to disable automatic failover, for example during maintenance.

Unpause the cluster to resume automatic failover.

You can find more information about that in pause.

Bootstrapping and standbys creation

How does Patroni create a primary Postgres node? What about a standby Postgres node?
By default, Patroni uses initdb to bootstrap a new cluster. It uses pg_basebackup to copy the leader and create standbys.
